Appendix D Fixed points via alternative notions of optimality In this section, we will review alternatives to performance optimality under which fixed points are incentivized. We will elaborate on the settings introduced in Section 8 and provide formal statements and proofs. To motivate the following, consider an expert AI that chooses its prediction to match its world model, but without explicitly considering the effect of its prediction. For instance, such cognition could arise in an AI trained via a purely supervised objective on historical data. This AI may not learn to take into account effects of its predictions on the outcome of the prediction. If it nevertheless has a world model that generalizes correctly to performative predictions, this could put the AI in a game in which it is trying to make a prediction to match its world model, while the world model updates its beliefs conditional on the AI’s prediction. The only equilibria of this game would be fixed points. Alternatively, fixed points could also result from different training schemes that explicitly optimize an AI’s prediction to track empirical outcomes, without also incentivizing influencing the outcomes themselves, such as repeated risk minimization or repeated gradient descent [ Perdomo et al. 2020 ] . Such expert AIs would likely be safer than ones optimizing for performative optimality. First, they report their true beliefs, which gives us better information to base decisions on. This also enables approaches in which we ensure that there is only one safe fixed point. Second, they do not explicitly optimize the choice of fixed point for a goal such as decreasing entropy. Instead, which fixed point is chosen will be contingent on initialization and specifics of the fixed point finding procedure. D.1 Performative stability and game theory We begin by defining performative stability and relating it to an equilibrium in a two-player game. This represents the core idea behind all of the following settings. A prediction 𝒑 ∗ {\bm{p}}^{*} is called performatively stable [ Perdomo et al. 2020 ] if 𝒑 ∗ ∈ arg ​ max 𝐩 ⁡ S ​ ( 𝐩 , f ⁡ ( 𝐩 ∗ ) ) . {\bm{p}}^{*}\in\argmax_{{\bm{p}}}S({\bm{p}},f({\bm{p}}^{*})). (16) First, it is clear that in our case, this is equivalent to 𝒑 ∗ {\bm{p}}^{*} being a fixed point. Proposition 5 . Assume S S is strictly proper. Then a prediction 𝐩 ∗ {\bm{p}}^{*} is a fixed point if and only if it is performatively stable. Proof. “ ⇒ \Rightarrow ”. Assume f ⁡ ( 𝒑 ∗ ) = 𝒑 ∗ f({\bm{p}}^{*})={\bm{p}}^{*} . Then S ( 𝒑 ∗ , f ( 𝒑 ∗ ) ) = S ( 𝒑 ∗ , 𝒑 ∗ ) ≥ S ( 𝒑 , 𝒑 ∗ ) = S ( 𝒑 , f ( 𝒑 ∗ ) ) S({\bm{p}}^{*},f({\bm{p}}*))=S({\bm{p}}^{*},{\bm{p}}^{*})\geq S({\bm{p}},{\bm{p}}^{*})=S({\bm{p}},f({\bm{p}}^{*})) for any 𝒑 {\bm{p}} since S S is proper. Hence, 𝒑 ∗ ∈ arg ​ max 𝐩 ⁡ S ​ ( 𝐩 , f ⁡ ( 𝐩 ∗ ) ) . {\bm{p}}^{*}\in\argmax_{{\bm{p}}}S({\bm{p}},f({\bm{p}}^{*})). “ ⇐ \Leftarrow ”. Assume 𝒑 ∗ ∈ arg ​ max 𝐩 ⁡ S ​ ( 𝐩 , f ⁡ ( 𝐩 ∗ ) ) {\bm{p}}^{*}\in\argmax_{{\bm{p}}}S({\bm{p}},f({\bm{p}}^{*})) . Then since S S is strictly proper, it must be 𝒑 ∗ = f ⁡ ( 𝒑 ∗ ) {\bm{p}}^{*}=f({\bm{p}}^{*}) . ∎ Next, the above objective is equivalent to the definition of a Nash equilibrium in the following game. Definition 2 (Oracle game) . Consider a two-player continuous game in which the first player controls 𝒑 ∈ Δ ⁡ ( 𝒩 ) {\bm{p}}\in\Delta(\mathcal{N}) and the second player controls 𝒒 ∈ Δ ⁡ ( 𝒩 ) \bm{q}\in\Delta(\mathcal{N}) , with payoff functions U 1 ​ ( 𝒑 , 𝒒 ) := S ⁡ ( 𝒑 , 𝒒 ) U_{1}({\bm{p}},\bm{q}):=S({\bm{p}},\bm{q}) and U 2 ​ ( 𝒑 , 𝒒 ) := S ⁡ ( 𝒒 , f ⁡ ( 𝒑 ) ) U_{2}({\bm{p}},\bm{q}):=S(\bm{q},f({\bm{p}})) for the two players, respectively. If 𝒑 ∗ , 𝒒 ∗ {\bm{p}}^{*},\bm{q}^{*} is a Nash equilibrium of the oracle game, we have p ∗ = arg ​ max 𝐩 ⁡ S ​ ( 𝐩 , 𝐪 ) p^{*}=\argmax_{\bm{p}}S({\bm{p}},\bm{q}) and 𝒒 ∗ = arg ​ max 𝐪 ⁡ S ​ ( 𝐪 , f ⁡ ( 𝐩 ∗ ) ) \bm{q}^{*}=\argmax_{\bm{q}}S(\bm{q},f({\bm{p}}^{*})) . Substituting the optimal value 𝒒 ∗ = f ⁡ ( 𝒑 ∗ ) \bm{q}^{*}=f({\bm{p}}^{*}) for the second player gives us exactly above definition of performative stability in Equation 16 . Conversely, if a prediction 𝒑 ∗ {\bm{p}}^{*} is performatively stable, then setting 𝒒 ∗ := f ⁡ ( 𝒑 ∗ ) \bm{q}^{*}:=f({\bm{p}}^{*}) yields a Nash equilibrium. Proposition 6 . Assume S S is a proper scoring rule. Then 𝐩 ∈ Δ ⁡ ( 𝒩 ) {\bm{p}}\in\Delta(\mathcal{N}) , 𝐪 := f ⁡ ( 𝐩 ) \bm{q}:=f({\bm{p}}) is a Nash equilibrium of the oracle game, if and only if 𝐩 {\bm{p}} is performatively stable. By Proposition 5 , this is equivalent to 𝐩 {\bm{p}} being a fixed point. The oracle game could arise in an agent that uses a causal decision theory [ Weirich 2020 ] to maximize its score and that believes that S S is influenced causally by 𝒑 {\bm{p}} , but only acausally by f ⁡ ( 𝒑 ) f({\bm{p}}) . In that case, the only ratifiable [ Jeffrey 1990 , Bell et al. 2021 , Ch. 1.7] decision is a Nash equilibrium of the above game. Similarly, the deliberational causal epistemic decision theory discussed by Greaves 2013 would output Nash equilibria of this game (whereas performative optimality would correspond to an agent using evidential epistemic decision theory in this case). Note that it is important that both players act simultaneously. Perdomo et al. 2020 introduce a Stackelberg version of the oracle game that produces performatively optimal instead of performatively stable reports. Consider a game in which player 1 1 acts first and chooses 𝒑 {\bm{p}} , after which player 2 2 responds with a prediction 𝒒 \bm{q} . Then player 2 2 responds 𝒒 = f ⁡ ( 𝒑 ) \bm{q}=f({\bm{p}}) to player 1 1 ’s action, and player 1 1 ’s optimization problem becomes p ∗ = arg ​ max 𝐩 ⁡ S ​ ( 𝐩 , arg ​ max 𝐪 ⁡ S ​ ( 𝐪 , f ⁡ ( 𝐩 ) ) ) = arg ​ max 𝐩 ⁡ S ​ ( 𝐩 , f ⁡ ( 𝐩 ) ) . p^{*}=\argmax_{{\bm{p}}}S({\bm{p}},\argmax_{\bm{q}}S(\bm{q},f({\bm{p}})))=\argmax_{\bm{p}}S({\bm{p}},f({\bm{p}})). D.2 Repeated risk minimization and repeated gradient descent Above, we have defined performative stability and a related game which yield fixed points, but we have not defined methods for solving these problems. In the performative prediction context, Perdomo et al. 2020 introduce repeated risk minimization and repeated gradient descent , both methods that converge to performatively stable points. In this section, we review both schemes and show how repeated gradient descent can be seen as gradient descent on a stop-gradient [ Foerster et al. 2018 , Demski 2019 ] objective. We assume direct access to 𝒒 \bm{q} , instead of having only access to samples distributed according to 𝒒 \bm{q} . In the next section, we discuss online learning when we only have access to samples. One way to understand this distinction is that the former corresponds to the internal cognition of an agent with a belief 𝒒 = f ⁡ ( 𝒑 ) \bm{q}=f({\bm{p}}) optimizing a prediction 𝒑 {\bm{p}} . The latter instead corresponds to a machine learning training setup for an oracle AI, where 𝒒 \bm{q} is the ground truth environment distribution instead of the oracle’s belief. Of course, there is no strict divide between the two. Any optimization algorithm could be used either by the agent itself or to train the agent. First, repeated risk minimization is a procedure by which we start with a prediction 𝒑 0 {\bm{p}}_{0} and then iteratively update the prediction as 𝒑 t + 1 = arg ​ max 𝐩 ⁡ S ​ ( 𝐩 , f ⁡ ( 𝐩 t ) ) {\bm{p}}_{t+1}=\argmax_{{\bm{p}}}S({\bm{p}},f({\bm{p}}_{t})) . This is also the same as alternating best response learning in the oracle game, where player 1 1 iteratively updates their prediction, responding to predictions 𝒒 t = f ⁡ ( 𝒑 t ) \bm{q}_{t}=f({\bm{p}}_{t}) from player 2 2 . If S S is strictly proper, 𝒑 t + 1 = f ⁡ ( 𝒑 t ) {\bm{p}}_{t+1}=f({\bm{p}}_{t}) , and this results in fixed point iteration for f f . Fixed point iteration converges globally to a fixed point if f f has Lipschitz constant L f &lt; 1 L_{f}&lt;1 . It also converges locally to a fixed point 𝒑 ∗ {\bm{p}}^{*} if f f is continuously differentiable at 𝒑 ∗ {\bm{p}}^{*} and ρ ⁡ ( D ​ f ​ ( 𝒑 ∗ ) ) &lt; 1 \rho(Df({\bm{p}}^{*}))&lt;1 , where ρ ⁡ ( D ​ f ​ ( 𝒑 ∗ ) ) \rho(Df({\bm{p}}^{*})) is the spectral radius of the Jacobian matrix D ​ f ​ ( 𝒑 ∗ ) Df({\bm{p}}^{*}) . Second, assume that S S is differentiable. Then repeated gradient ascent updates points via 𝒑 t + 1 := Π Δ ​ ( 𝒑 t + α ​ 𝔼 y ∼ f ⁡ ( 𝒑 t ) ​ [ ∇ 𝒑 S ​ ( 𝒑 t , y ) ] ) , {\bm{p}}_{t+1}:=\Pi_{\Delta}({\bm{p}}_{t}+\alpha\mathbb{E}_{y\sim f({\bm{p}}_{t})}[\nabla_{\bm{p}}S({\bm{p}}_{t},y)]), where Π Δ \Pi_{\Delta} is the Euclidean projection onto the probability simplex Δ ⁡ ( 𝒩 ) \Delta(\mathcal{N}) , and α &gt; 0 \alpha&gt;0 is the learning rate. Using the definition of S ⁡ ( 𝒑 , 𝒒 ) S({\bm{p}},\bm{q}) , we have 𝔼 y ∼ f ⁡ ( 𝒑 t ) ​ [ ∇ 𝒑 S ​ ( 𝒑 t , y ) ] = ∇ 𝒑 ( 𝔼 y ∼ 𝒒 ​ [ S ⁡ ( 𝒑 t , y ) ] ) | 𝒒 = f ⁡ ( 𝒑 t ) = ∇ 𝒑 ( S ⁡ ( 𝒑 t , 𝒒 ) ) | 𝒒 = f ⁡ ( 𝒑 ) \mathbb{E}_{y\sim f({\bm{p}}_{t})}[\nabla_{\bm{p}}S({\bm{p}}_{t},y)]=\nabla_{\bm{p}}(\mathbb{E}_{y\sim\bm{q}}[S({\bm{p}}_{t},y)])|_{\bm{q}=f({\bm{p}}_{t})}=\nabla_{\bm{p}}(S({\bm{p}}_{t},\bm{q}))|_{\bm{q}=f({\bm{p}})} We can express this as ∇ 𝒑 ( S ⁡ ( 𝒑 t , ⊥ f ⁡ ( 𝒑 t ) ) ) := ∇ 𝒑 ( S ⁡ ( 𝒑 t , 𝒒 ) ) | 𝒒 = f ⁡ ( 𝒑 ) , \nabla_{\bm{p}}(S({\bm{p}}_{t},\bot f({\bm{p}}_{t}))):=\nabla_{\bm{p}}(S({\bm{p}}_{t},\bm{q}))|_{\bm{q}=f({\bm{p}})}, where ⊥ \bot is the stop-gradient operator , which evaluates to the identity function but sets gradients to zero, ∇ x ( ⊥ x ) = 0 \nabla_{x}(\bot x)=0 [ Foerster et al. 2018 , Demski 2019 ] . 4 4 4 This is not a mathematical function (there is no function that is equal to the identity but has gradient zero everywhere), but rather a notational convention in reference to the stop_gradient or detach functions from the tensorflow or pytorch python libraries. Interestingly, one can perform valid derivations using the stop-gradient operator (e.g., using the chain rule). We leave it to future work to explore the mathematics behind stop-gradients further. In the following, we call S ⁡ ( 𝒑 , ⊥ f ⁡ ( 𝒑 ) ) S({\bm{p}},\bot f({\bm{p}})) the stop-gradient objective . Importantly, it matters that the gradient in repeated gradient ascent lies inside instead of outside the expectation: OPEN 𝔼 y ∼ f ⁡ ( 𝒑 t ) ​ [ ∇ 𝒑 S ​ ( 𝒑 t , y ) ] = ∇ 𝒑 ( S ⁡ ( 𝒑 t , ⊥ f ⁡ ( 𝒑 t ) ) ) ≠ ∇ p ( S ⁡ ( 𝒑 t , f ⁡ ( 𝒑 t ) ) ) = ∇ 𝒑 𝔼 y ∼ f ⁡ ( 𝒑 t ) ​ [ S ⁡ ( 𝒑 t , y ) ] ) . \mathbb{E}_{y\sim f({\bm{p}}_{t})}[\nabla_{\bm{p}}S({\bm{p}}_{t},y)]=\nabla_{\bm{p}}(S({\bm{p}}_{t},\bot f({\bm{p}}_{t})))\neq\nabla_{p}(S({\bm{p}}_{t},f({\bm{p}}_{t})))=\nabla_{\bm{p}}\mathbb{E}_{y\sim f({\bm{p}}_{t})}[S({\bm{p}}_{t},y)]). Unlike repeated gradient ascent, the latter implements gradient ascent on S ⁡ ( 𝒑 , f ⁡ ( 𝒑 ) ) S({\bm{p}},f({\bm{p}})) and thus leads to performatively optimal reports. Perdomo et al. 2020 show that, given their assumptions, repeated gradient descent globally converges to stable fixed points. They also provide convergence rates. We will show an analogous result relating repeated gradient ascent to fixed points in our setting, though we won’t analyze global convergence or rates of convergence. To begin, we show that repeated gradient descent is equivalent to Naive Learning [ Letcher et al. 2019 ] in the oracle game, assuming that player 2 2 always plays 𝒒 = f ⁡ ( 𝒑 ) \bm{q}=f({\bm{p}}) . Proposition 7 . Assume player 1 1 is performing gradient ascent on its objective with learning rate α \alpha , under the assumption that player 2 2 always plays 𝐪 = f ⁡ ( 𝐩 ) \bm{q}=f({\bm{p}}) . Then player 1 1 ’s update is 𝒑 t + 1 = Π Δ ​ ( 𝒑 t + α ​ ∇ 𝒑 ( 𝐒 ⁡ ( 𝒑 t , ⊥ f ⁡ ( 𝒑 t ) ) ) ) . {\bm{p}}_{t+1}=\Pi_{\Delta}({\bm{p}}_{t}+\alpha\nabla_{\bm{p}}(\mathbf{S}({\bm{p}}_{t},\bot f({\bm{p}}_{t})))). Proof. The proof follows immediately from the definitions. Player 1 1 ’s update is, by assumption, 𝒑 t + 1 = Π Δ ​ ( 𝒑 t + α ​ ∇ 𝒑 ( U 1 ​ ( 𝒑 t , 𝒒 ) ) ) = Π Δ ​ ( 𝒑 t + α ​ ∇ 𝒑 ( S ⁡ ( 𝒑 t , 𝒒 ) ) ) {\bm{p}}_{t+1}=\Pi_{\Delta}({\bm{p}}_{t}+\alpha\nabla_{\bm{p}}(U_{1}({\bm{p}}_{t},\bm{q})))=\Pi_{\Delta}({\bm{p}}_{t}+\alpha\nabla_{\bm{p}}(S({\bm{p}}_{t},\bm{q}))) where 𝒒 \bm{q} is player 2 2 ’s action. Assuming player 2 2 plays 𝒒 = f ⁡ ( 𝒑 t ) \bm{q}=f({\bm{p}}_{t}) , we get 𝒑 t + 1 = Π Δ ​ ( 𝒑 t + α ​ ∇ 𝒑 ( S ⁡ ( 𝒑 t , 𝒒 ) ) ) = Π Δ ​ ( 𝒑 t + α ​ ∇ p ( S ⁡ ( 𝒑 t , ⊥ f ⁡ ( 𝒑 t ) ) ) ) {\bm{p}}_{t+1}=\Pi_{\Delta}({\bm{p}}_{t}+\alpha\nabla_{\bm{p}}(S({\bm{p}}_{t},\bm{q})))=\Pi_{\Delta}({\bm{p}}_{t}+\alpha\nabla_{p}(S({\bm{p}}_{t},\bot f({\bm{p}}_{t})))) ∎ Next, we show that fixed points are critical points of the stop-gradient objective. Proposition 8 . Assume S S is proper and let G , g G,g as in the Gneiting and Raftery characterization of S S ( Theorem 1 ) be differentiable. Then for any 𝐩 ∈ Δ ⁡ ( 𝒩 ) {\bm{p}}\in\Delta(\mathcal{N}) , we have ∇ 𝒑 ( S ⁡ ( 𝒑 , ⊥ f ⁡ ( 𝒑 ) ) ) = D ​ g ​ ( 𝒑 ) ⊤ ​ ( f ⁡ ( 𝒑 ) − 𝒑 ) . \nabla_{\bm{p}}(S({\bm{p}},\bot f({\bm{p}})))=Dg({\bm{p}})^{\top}(f({\bm{p}})-{\bm{p}}). The reverse is true if D ​ g ​ ( 𝐩 ) | 𝒯 ≻ 0 Dg({\bm{p}})|_{\mathcal{T}}\succ 0 . Proof. ∇ 𝒑 ( S ⁡ ( 𝒑 , ⊥ f ⁡ ( 𝒑 ) ) ) = ∇ 𝒑 ( S ⁡ ( 𝒑 , 𝒒 ) ) | 𝒒 = f ⁡ ( 𝒑 ) = ∇ 𝒑 ( G ⁡ ( 𝒑 ) + g ​ ( 𝒑 ) ⊤ ​ ( 𝒒 − 𝒑 ) ) | 𝒒 = f ⁡ ( 𝒑 ) = ( g ⁡ ( 𝒑 ) + D ​ g ​ ( 𝒑 ) ⊤ ​ ( 𝒒 − 𝒑 ) − g ⁡ ( 𝒑 ) ) | 𝒒 = f ⁡ ( 𝒑 ) = D ​ g ​ ( 𝒑 ) ⊤ ​ ( f ⁡ ( 𝒑 ) − 𝒑 ) . \nabla_{\bm{p}}(S({\bm{p}},\bot f({\bm{p}})))=\nabla_{\bm{p}}(S({\bm{p}},\bm{q}))|_{\bm{q}=f({\bm{p}})}=\nabla_{\bm{p}}(G({\bm{p}})+g({\bm{p}})^{\top}(\bm{q}-{\bm{p}}))|_{\bm{q}=f({\bm{p}})}\\ =(g({\bm{p}})+Dg({\bm{p}})^{\top}(\bm{q}-{\bm{p}})-g({\bm{p}}))|_{\bm{q}=f({\bm{p}})}=Dg({\bm{p}})^{\top}(f({\bm{p}})-{\bm{p}}). (17) If 𝒑 {\bm{p}} is a fixed point, it follows that ∇ 𝒑 ( S ⁡ ( 𝒑 , ⊥ f ⁡ ( 𝒑 ) ) ) = 0 \nabla_{\bm{p}}(S({\bm{p}},\bot f({\bm{p}})))=0 . Moreover, if D ​ g ​ ( 𝒑 ) | 𝒯 ≻ 0 Dg({\bm{p}})|_{\mathcal{T}}\succ 0 , then if f ⁡ ( 𝒑 ) − 𝒑 ≠ 0 , f({\bm{p}})-{\bm{p}}\neq 0, ∇ 𝒑 ( S ⁡ ( 𝒑 , ⊥ f ⁡ ( 𝒑 ) ) ) ⊤ ​ ( f ⁡ ( 𝒑 ) − 𝒑 ) = ( f ⁡ ( 𝒑 ) − 𝒑 ) ⊤ ​ D ​ g ​ ( 𝒑 ) ​ ( f ⁡ ( 𝒑 ) − 𝒑 ) &gt; 0 \nabla_{\bm{p}}(S({\bm{p}},\bot f({\bm{p}})))^{\top}(f({\bm{p}})-{\bm{p}})=(f({\bm{p}})-{\bm{p}})^{\top}Dg({\bm{p}})(f({\bm{p}})-{\bm{p}})&gt;0 and thus ∇ 𝒑 ( S ⁡ ( 𝒑 , ⊥ f ⁡ ( 𝒑 ) ) ) ≠ 0 \nabla_{\bm{p}}(S({\bm{p}},\bot f({\bm{p}})))\neq 0 . ∎ Finally, we show that in our setting, repeated gradient ascent locally converges to fixed points 𝒑 ∗ {\bm{p}}^{*} , assuming that ‖ D ​ f ​ ( 𝒑 ∗ ) ‖ op \|Df({\bm{p}}^{*})\|_{\mathrm{op}} is sufficiently small. This is a local version of convergence results from Perdomo et al. 2020 , adapted to our setting. Proposition 9 . Let S S be a strictly proper scoring rule. Let 𝐩 ∗ ∈ int ⁡ ( Δ ⁡ ( 𝒩 ) ) {\bm{p}}^{*}\in\mathrm{int}(\Delta(\mathcal{N})) be a fixed point of f f such that G G is three times differentiable at 𝐩 ∗ {\bm{p}}^{*} , i.e. D 2 g ( 𝐩 ∗ ) = D 2 ∇ g ( 𝐩 ∗ ) D^{2}g({\bm{p}}^{*})=D^{2}\nabla g({\bm{p}}^{*}) exists. Proof of Proposition 9 . The Banach space we consider will be 𝒯 \mathcal{T} . (20) Here, D 2 ​ g ​ ( 𝒗 + 𝒑 ∗ ) D^{2}g(\bm{v}+{\bm{p}}^{*}) is a third-degree tensor, and D 2 ​ g ​ ( 𝒗 + 𝒑 ∗ ) ​ [ f ⁡ ( 𝒑 ∗ ) − 𝒑 ∗ ] D^{2}g(\bm{v}+{\bm{p}}^{*})[f({\bm{p}}^{*})-{\bm{p}}^{*}] is a linear map. Since f ⁡ ( 𝒑 ∗ ) = 𝒑 ∗ f({\bm{p}}^{*})={\bm{p}}^{*} , it follows D ​ φ ​ ( 0 ) = I + α ​ D ​ g ​ ( 𝒑 ∗ ) ⊤ ​ ( D ​ f ​ ( 𝒑 ∗ ) − I ) D\varphi(0)=I+\alpha Dg({\bm{p}}^{*})^{\top}(Df({\bm{p}}^{*})-I) . In particular, φ \varphi is differentiable at 0 0 . Now let 𝒗 \bm{v} be an arbitrary eigenvector of D ​ φ ​ ( 0 ) D\varphi(0) , with eigenvalue λ \lambda and w.l.o.g. assume ‖ 𝒗 ‖ = 1 \|\bm{v}\|=1 . Note that 𝒗 ⊤ ​ D ​ g ​ ( 𝒑 ∗ ) ​ 𝒗 ≥ γ ​ ‖ 𝒗 ‖ = γ \bm{v}^{\top}Dg({\bm{p}}^{*})\bm{v}\geq\gamma\|\bm{v}\|=\gamma and 𝒗 ⊤ ​ D ​ g ​ ( 𝒑 ∗ ) ​ 𝒗 ≤ β ​ ‖ 𝒗 ‖ ≤ β \bm{v}^{\top}Dg({\bm{p}}^{*})\bm{v}\leq\beta\|\bm{v}\|\leq\beta by assumption. (21) This shows that ρ ⁡ ( D ​ φ ​ ( 0 ) ) &lt; 1 \rho(D\varphi(0))&lt;1 . Hence, by Theorem 8 , we can conclude that there exists an open set U ⊆ 𝒟 U\subseteq\mathcal{D} such that for arbitrary 𝒗 0 ∈ U \bm{v}_{0}\in U , 𝒗 t := φ ⁡ ( 𝒗 t − 1 ) ∈ U \bm{v}_{t}:=\varphi(\bm{v}_{t-1})\in U for all t ≥ 1 t\geq 1 , and lim t → ∞ 𝒗 t = 0 \lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}\bm{v}_{t}=0 . In particular, note that since 𝒗 t ∈ U \bm{v}_{t}\in U for all t t , 𝒗 t + 𝒑 ∗ ∈ Δ ⁡ ( 𝒩 ) \bm{v}_{t}+{\bm{p}}^{*}\in\Delta(\mathcal{N}) and 𝒑 ∗ + 𝒗 t + 1 = 𝒑 ∗ + 𝒗 t + α ​ ∇ 𝒗 ( S ⁡ ( 𝒗 t + 𝒑 ∗ , ⊥ f ⁡ ( 𝒗 t + 𝒑 ∗ ) ) ) = Π Δ ​ ( 𝒑 ∗ + 𝒗 t + α ​ ∇ 𝒗 ( S ⁡ ( 𝒗 t + 𝒑 ∗ , ⊥ f ⁡ ( 𝒗 t + 𝒑 ∗ ) ) ) ) {\bm{p}}^{*}+\bm{v}_{t+1}={\bm{p}}^{*}+\bm{v}_{t}+\alpha\nabla_{\bm{v}}(S(\bm{v}_{t}+{\bm{p}}^{*},\bot f(\bm{v}_{t}+{\bm{p}}^{*})))=\Pi_{\Delta}({\bm{p}}^{*}+\bm{v}_{t}+\alpha\nabla_{\bm{v}}(S(\bm{v}_{t}+{\bm{p}}^{*},\bot f(\bm{v}_{t}+{\bm{p}}^{*})))) for all t t . Hence, setting 𝒑 t := 𝒑 ∗ + 𝒗 t {\bm{p}}_{t}:={\bm{p}}^{*}+\bm{v}_{t} , it follows 𝒑 t + 1 = Π Δ ​ ( 𝒑 t + α ​ ∇ 𝒑 ( 𝐒 ⁡ ( 𝒑 t , ⊥ f ⁡ ( 𝒑 t ) ) ) ) {\bm{p}}_{t+1}=\Pi_{\Delta}({\bm{p}}_{t}+\alpha\nabla_{\bm{p}}(\mathbf{S}({\bm{p}}_{t},\bot f({\bm{p}}_{t})))) for all t t and lim t → ∞ 𝒑 t = 𝒑 ∗ + lim t → ∞ 𝒗 t = 𝒑 ∗ . \lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}{\bm{p}}_{t}={\bm{p}}^{*}+\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}\bm{v}_{t}={\bm{p}}^{*}. This concludes the proof. ∎ D.3 Online learning Now consider a machine learning setup in which we train an oracle with stochastic gradient ascent on environment samples. We assume that at time t t , a model makes a prediction 𝑷 t \bm{P}_{t} and receives a score S ⁡ ( 𝑷 t , Y t ) S(\bm{P}_{t},Y_{t}) , where Y t ∼ f ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ) Y_{t}\sim f(\bm{P}_{t}) . The model is then updated using gradient ascent on S ⁡ ( 𝑷 t , Y t ) S(\bm{P}_{t},Y_{t}) . That is, for some learning rate schedule ( α t ) t (\alpha_{t})_{t} , we have 𝑷 t + 1 = Π Δ ​ ( 𝑷 t + α t ​ ∇ 𝒑 S ​ ( 𝑷 t , Y t ) ) , \bm{P}_{t+1}=\Pi_{\Delta}(\bm{P}_{t}+\alpha_{t}\nabla_{\bm{p}}S(\bm{P}_{t},Y_{t})), where Π Δ \Pi_{\Delta} is the Euclidean projection onto Δ ⁡ ( 𝒩 ) \Delta(\mathcal{N}) as before. We discuss this as a theoretical model for oracles trained using machine learning, to show how training setups may incentivize predicting fixed points. There are many issues with the setting beyond giving accurate predictions; for instance, learning may fail to converge at all, and even if the training process sets the right incentives on training examples, the learned model may be optimizing a different objective when generalizing to new predictions [ Hubinger et al. 2019 ] . To see that this setting leads to fixed points, note that we have 𝔼 Y t ∼ f ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ) ​ [ ∇ 𝒑 S ​ ( 𝑷 t , Y t ) ] = ∇ 𝒑 𝔼 Y t ∼ ⊥ f ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ) ​ [ S ⁡ ( 𝑷 t , Y t ) ] = ∇ 𝒑 ( S ⁡ ( 𝑷 t , ⊥ f ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ) ) ) . \mathbb{E}_{Y_{t}\sim f(\bm{P}_{t})}[\nabla_{\bm{p}}S(\bm{P}_{t},Y_{t})]=\nabla_{\bm{p}}\mathbb{E}_{Y_{t}\sim\bot f(\bm{P}_{t})}[S(\bm{P}_{t},Y_{t})]=\nabla_{\bm{p}}(S(\bm{P}_{t},\bot f(\bm{P}_{t}))). That is, the expectation of this gradient, conditional on 𝑷 t , \bm{P}_{t}, is exactly the repeated gradient from the previous section. Hence, given the right assumptions, this converges to fixed points instead of performative optima. We do not show this here, but an analogous result in performative prediction was proved by Mendler-Dünner et al. 2020 . There are several variations of this setup that essentially set the same incentives. For instance, one could also draw entire batches of outcomes Y t , 1 : B Y_{t,1:B} and then perform updates based on the batch gradient ∇ 𝒑 ∑ b = 1 B S ( 𝑷 t , Y t , b ) . \nabla_{\bm{p}}\sum_{b=1}^{B}S(\bm{P}_{t},Y_{t,b}). This is a Monte Carlo estimate of the repeated gradient and hence also converges to performatively stable points and thus fixed points [ Perdomo et al. 2020 ] . One could also mix the two algorithms and, e.g., perform gradient ascent on an average of past losses, yielding a version of the backwards-facing oracle discussed in Armstrong 2018 . Note that finding fixed points depends on the fact that we differentiate S ⁡ ( 𝑷 t , Y t ) S(\bm{P}_{t},Y_{t}) instead of the expectation 𝔼 Y t ∼ f ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ) ​ [ S ⁡ ( 𝑷 t , Y t ) ] = S ⁡ ( 𝑷 t , f ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ) ) \mathbb{E}_{Y_{t}\sim f(\bm{P}_{t})}[S(\bm{P}_{t},Y_{t})]=S(\bm{P}_{t},f(\bm{P}_{t})) . If we used policy gradients to differentiate S ⁡ ( 𝑷 t , f ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ) ) S(\bm{P}_{t},f(\bm{P}_{t})) , for instance, we would again optimize for performative optimality. Similarly, we could learn a Q-function representing scores for each prediction, and update the function based on randomly sampled predictions 𝒑 {\bm{p}} . Then the Q-function would converge to estimates of S ⁡ ( 𝒑 , f ⁡ ( 𝒑 ) ) S({\bm{p}},f({\bm{p}})) , and the highest Q-value prediction would be a performative optimum. There are also some more recent results in performative prediction that explicitly try to estimate the gradient ∇ 𝒑 ( S ⁡ ( 𝒑 , f ⁡ ( 𝒑 ) ) ) \nabla_{\bm{p}}(S({\bm{p}},f({\bm{p}}))) and thus find performatively optimal instead of stable points [ Izzo et al. 2021 ] . Stop-gradients could also be circumvented in a hidden way [ Krueger et al. 2020 ] . For instance, consider a hyperparameter search to meta-learn a learning algorithm, where the evaluation criterion is the accumulated score during an episode. Then this search would prefer algorithms that optimize S ⁡ ( 𝒑 , f ⁡ ( 𝒑 ) ) S({\bm{p}},f({\bm{p}})) directly, without a stop-gradient. Lastly, repeated gradient descent is related to decoupled approval in RL Uesato et al. 2020 . The decoupled approval policy gradient samples actions and approval queries independently and can thus differentiate with a stop-gradient in front of the approval signal. In our setting, we can differentiate through S ⁡ ( 𝑷 t , Y t ) S(\bm{P}_{t},Y_{t}) directly, so it is not necessary to calculate this gradient with a decoupled policy gradient. Decoupled gradients could be used to implement the stop-gradient objective if scores were discrete or otherwise not differentiable. D.4 No-regret learning In this section, we consider no-regret learning and show that algorithms have sublinear regret if and only if their prediction error is sublinear. Regret takes environment outcomes as given and asks which predictions would have been optimal in hindsight. It thus corresponds to an alternative notion of optimality with a “stop-gradient” in front of environment probabilities. As in the previous section, we assume that at time t ∈ ℕ , t\in\mathbb{N}, the agent (i.e., the oracle AI) makes a prediction 𝑷 t \bm{P}_{t} and receives a score S ⁡ ( 𝑷 t , Y t ) S(\bm{P}_{t},Y_{t}) , where Y t ∼ f ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ) Y_{t}\sim f(\bm{P}_{t}) . The agent’s cumulative score at step T T is defined as ∑ t = 1 T S ⁡ ( 𝑷 t , Y t ) \sum_{t=1}^{T}S(\bm{P}_{t},Y_{t}) . In no-regret learning, we compare performance against experts , which choose sequences of probabilities ( 𝑷 t ′ ) t , (\bm{P}^{\prime}_{t})_{t}, 𝑷 t ′ ∈ Δ ⁡ ( 𝒩 ) \bm{P}^{\prime}_{t}\in\Delta(\mathcal{N}) . We assume that an expert’s prediction 𝑷 t ′ \bm{P}_{t}^{\prime} is independent of Y t Y_{t} conditional on 𝑷 t \bm{P}_{t} . I.e., an expert knows the predictions 𝑷 t \bm{P}_{t} and thus probabilities f ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ) f(\bm{P}_{t}) , but it does not know the outcome of Y t Y_{t} . Let 𝒫 \mathcal{P} be the set of all such experts. The regret of the agent is the difference between the cumulative score received by the best expert in expectation and the cumulative score received by the agent. To define it formally, let 𝑷 t ∗ ∈ arg ​ max 𝐏 t ′ ∈ 𝒫 ⁡ 𝔼 ​ [ S ⁡ ( 𝐏 t ′ , Y t ) ∣ 𝐏 t ] \bm{P}^{*}_{t}\in\argmax_{\bm{P}^{\prime}_{t}\in\mathcal{P}}\mathbb{E}[S(\bm{P}^{\prime}_{t},Y_{t})\mid\bm{P}_{t}] for t ∈ ℕ t\in\mathbb{N} . 𝑷 t ∗ \bm{P}^{*}_{t} is a random variable that maximizes the expectation of S ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ∗ , Y t ) S(\bm{P}^{*}_{t},Y_{t}) before Y t Y_{t} is drawn, but conditional on 𝑷 t \bm{P}_{t} . Definition 3 (Regret) . The regret of agent ( 𝑷 t ) t (\bm{P}_{t})_{t} at time T T is Regret ⁡ ( T ) ≔ ∑ t = 1 T S ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ∗ , Y t ) − S ⁡ ( 𝑷 t , Y t ) . \mathrm{Regret}(T)\coloneqq\sum_{t=1}^{T}S(\bm{P}_{t}^{*},Y_{t})-S(\bm{P}_{t},Y_{t}). The agent is said to have sublinear regret or no-regret if lim sup T → ∞ 1 T ​ Regret ​ ( T ) ≤ 0 . \limsup_{T\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{T}\mathrm{Regret}(T)\leq 0. First, note that we define regret relative to the best expert in expectation instead of the best expert in hindsight. The latter would always be the one that made confident predictions and accidentally got all predictions exactly right. We are interested in algorithms with sublinear regret, and for that purpose it would be too much to ask the agent to perform well compared to the best expert in hindsight. Moreover, for scoring rules that are symmetric between the outcomes, this expert would have a constant score C C . This would imply that Regret ​ ( T ) = ∑ t = 1 T C − S ⁡ ( 𝑷 t , Y t ) \text{Regret}(T)=\sum_{t=1}^{T}C-S(\bm{P}_{t},Y_{t}) and reduce the problem to minimizing the negative score, which would lead to performatively optimal predictions. Second, we evaluate the performance of the expert with respect to the environment outcomes Y t Y_{t} generated by the agent ( 𝑷 t ) t (\bm{P}_{t})_{t} , instead of evaluating the expert according to outcomes Y ~ t ∼ f ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ∗ ) \tilde{Y}_{t}\sim f(\bm{P}^{*}_{t}) generated using the expert’s own predictions. This means that, to receive sublinear regret, the agent only has to make accurate predictions—it does not have to find a performatively optimal prediction. This is different from the no-regret learning setup discussed in Jagadeesan et al. 2022 , where regret is defined with respect to S ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ∗ , f ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ∗ ) ) S(\bm{P}^{*}_{t},f(\bm{P}^{*}_{t})) . In that setting, only agents converging to performatively optimal predictions have sublinear regret. We begin by showing that the best expert in expectation actually exists, and that 𝑷 t ∗ = f ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ) \bm{P}^{*}_{t}=f(\bm{P}_{t}) . Proposition 10 . Let S S be a proper scoring rule and ( 𝐏 t ′ ) t ∈ 𝒫 (\bm{P}_{t}^{\prime})_{t}\in\mathcal{P} an expert. Then for any t ∈ ℕ t\in\mathbb{N} , we have 𝔼 ⁡ [ S ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ′ , Y t ) ] = 𝔼 ⁡ [ S ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ′ , f ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ) ) ] . \mathbb{E}[S(\bm{P}_{t}^{\prime},Y_{t})]=\mathbb{E}[S(\bm{P}_{t}^{\prime},f(\bm{P}_{t}))]. Moreover, we have ( 𝐏 t ∗ ) t = ( f ⁡ ( 𝐏 t ) ) t (\bm{P}^{*}_{t})_{t}=(f(\bm{P}_{t}))_{t} and thus Regret ⁡ ( T ) = ∑ t = 1 T S ⁡ ( f ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ) , Y t ) − S ⁡ ( 𝑷 t , Y t ) . \mathrm{Regret}(T)=\sum_{t=1}^{T}S(f(\bm{P}_{t}),Y_{t})-S(\bm{P}_{t},Y_{t}). Proof. Let t ∈ ℕ t\in\mathbb{N} and let ( 𝑷 t ′ ) t ∈ 𝒫 (\bm{P}_{t}^{\prime})_{t}\in\mathcal{P} be any expert. Conditional on 𝑷 t \bm{P}_{t} , Y t ∼ f ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ) Y_{t}\sim f(\bm{P}_{t}) and Y t Y_{t} is independent of 𝑷 t ′ \bm{P}_{t}^{\prime} by assumption. Hence, 𝔼 [ S ( 𝑷 t ′ , Y t ) ] = 𝔼 [ 𝔼 [ S ( 𝑷 t ′ , Y t ) ∣ 𝑷 t , 𝑷 t ′ ] ] = 𝔼 [ S ( 𝑷 t ′ , f ( 𝑷 t ) ) ] . \mathbb{E}\left[S(\bm{P}_{t}^{\prime},Y_{t})\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}[S(\bm{P}_{t}^{\prime},Y_{t})\mid\bm{P}_{t},\bm{P}_{t}^{\prime}]\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[S(\bm{P}_{t}^{\prime},f(\bm{P}_{t}))\right]. Next, since S S is proper, 𝔼 ⁡ [ S ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ′ , f ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ) ) ] ≤ 𝔼 ⁡ [ S ⁡ ( f ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ) , f ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ) ) ] . \mathbb{E}\left[S(\bm{P}_{t}^{\prime},f(\bm{P}_{t}))\right]\leq\mathbb{E}\left[S(f(\bm{P}_{t}),f(\bm{P}_{t}))\right]. It follows that max ( 𝑷 t ′ ) t ∈ 𝒫 ⁡ 𝔼 ⁡ [ S ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ′ , Y t ) ] = max ( 𝑷 t ′ ) t ∈ 𝒫 ⁡ 𝔼 ⁡ [ S ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ′ , f ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ) ) ] ≤ 𝔼 ⁡ [ S ⁡ ( f ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ) , f ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ) ) ] = 𝔼 ⁡ [ S ⁡ ( f ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ) , Y t ) ] . \max_{(\bm{P}^{\prime}_{t})_{t}\in\mathcal{P}}\mathbb{E}\left[S(\bm{P}_{t}^{\prime},Y_{t})\right]=\max_{(\bm{P}^{\prime}_{t})_{t}\in\mathcal{P}}\mathbb{E}\left[S(\bm{P}_{t}^{\prime},f(\bm{P}_{t}))\right]\leq\mathbb{E}\left[S(f(\bm{P}_{t}),f(\bm{P}_{t}))\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[S(f(\bm{P}_{t}),Y_{t})\right]. Moreover, ( f ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ) ) t ∈ 𝒫 (f(\bm{P}_{t}))_{t}\in\mathcal{P} , as f ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ) f(\bm{P}_{t}) is constant given 𝑷 t \bm{P}_{t} and thus independent of Y t Y_{t} . It follows that, for any t ∈ ℕ , t\in\mathbb{N}, 𝑷 t ∗ ∈ arg ​ max ( 𝐏 t ′ ) t ∈ 𝒫 ⁡ 𝔼 ​ [ S ⁡ ( 𝐏 t ′ , Y t ) ] \bm{P}^{*}_{t}\in\argmax_{(\bm{P}_{t}^{\prime})_{t}\in\mathcal{P}}\mathbb{E}[S(\bm{P}^{\prime}_{t},Y_{t})] , and thus Regret ⁡ ( T ) = ∑ t = 1 T S ⁡ ( f ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ) , Y t ) − S ⁡ ( 𝑷 t , Y t ) . \mathrm{Regret}(T)=\sum_{t=1}^{T}S(f(\bm{P}_{t}),Y_{t})-S(\bm{P}_{t},Y_{t}). ∎ D.4.1 Characterization of regret in the limit If S S is unbounded (such as the log scoring rule), then the agent’s scores can become arbitrarily low, and the limit of 1 T ​ Regret ​ ( T ) \frac{1}{T}\mathrm{Regret}(T) may be undefined. To simplify our analysis, we will thus assume that there is a bound on the variance of the received score S ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ′ , Y t ) S(\bm{P}^{\prime}_{t},Y_{t}) and on the expected score S ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ′ , f ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ) ) S(\bm{P}^{\prime}_{t},f(\bm{P}_{t})) of both the agent, 𝑷 t ′ = 𝑷 t \bm{P}^{\prime}_{t}=\bm{P}_{t} , and the best expert, 𝑷 t ′ = 𝑷 t ∗ \bm{P}^{\prime}_{t}=\bm{P}^{*}_{t} . In the case of the log scoring rule, this would be satisfied, for instance, if the agent’s predictions are bounded away from the boundary of the probability simplex. Our next proposition shows that, given these assumptions, lim T → ∞ 1 T ​ Regret ​ ( T ) \lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{T}\mathrm{Regret}(T) exists and is nonnegative, and having sublinear regret is equivalent to lim t → ∞ 1 T ​ Regret ​ ( T ) = 0 . \lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{T}\mathrm{Regret}(T)=0. Proposition 11 . Let S S be a proper scoring rule. Assume that sup t | S ⁡ ( 𝐏 t ′ , f ⁡ ( 𝐏 t ) ) | &lt; ∞ \sup_{t}|S(\bm{P}^{\prime}_{t},f(\bm{P}_{t}))|&lt;\infty and that sup t Var ⁡ ( S ⁡ ( 𝐏 t ′ , Y t ) ) &lt; ∞ \sup_{t}\mathrm{Var}(S(\bm{P}^{\prime}_{t},Y_{t}))&lt;\infty for 𝐏 t ′ ∈ { 𝐏 t , f ⁡ ( 𝐏 t ) } \bm{P}^{\prime}_{t}\in\{\bm{P}_{t},f(\bm{P}_{t})\} . Then almost surely lim T → ∞ 1 T ​ Regret ​ ( T ) = lim T → ∞ 1 T ​ ∑ t = 1 T S ⁡ ( f ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ) , f ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ) ) − S ⁡ ( 𝑷 t , f ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ) ) ≥ 0 . \lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{T}\mathrm{Regret}(T)=\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}S(f(\bm{P}_{t}),f(\bm{P}_{t}))-S(\bm{P}_{t},f(\bm{P}_{t}))\geq 0. In particular, almost surely both limits exist and are finite, and the agent has sublinear regret if and only if lim T → ∞ 1 T ​ ∑ t = 1 T S ⁡ ( f ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ) , f ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ) ) − S ⁡ ( 𝑷 t , f ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ) ) = 0 . \lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}S(f(\bm{P}_{t}),f(\bm{P}_{t}))-S(\bm{P}_{t},f(\bm{P}_{t}))=0. Proof. We will use a version of the strong law of large numbers for uncorrelated random variables with bounded variance, adapted from Neely 2021 . Theorem 9 ( Neely 2021 , Theorem 2) . Let { X t } t ∈ ℕ 0 \{X_{t}\}_{t\in\mathbb{N}_{0}} be a sequence of pairwise uncorrelated random variables with mean 0 0 and bounded variances. I.e., assume that 1. 𝔼 ⁡ [ X t ] = 0 \mathbb{E}[X_{t}]=0 for all t ∈ ℕ 0 t\in\mathbb{N}_{0} 2. There exists c &gt; 0 c&gt;0 such that Var ⁡ ( X t ) ≤ c \mathrm{Var}(X_{t})\leq c for all t ∈ ℕ 0 t\in\mathbb{N}_{0} 3. Cov ⁡ ( X t , X t ′ ) = 0 \mathrm{Cov}(X_{t},X_{t^{\prime}})=0 for all t ≠ t ′ ∈ ℕ 0 t\neq t^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}_{0} . Then almost surely lim T → ∞ 1 T ​ ∑ t = 1 T X t = 0 . \lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}X_{t}=0. We will apply this law to random variables X t := S ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ′ , Y t ) − S ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ′ , f ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ) ) X_{t}:=S(\bm{P}^{\prime}_{t},Y_{t})-S(\bm{P}^{\prime}_{t},f(\bm{P}_{t})) , where 𝑷 t ′ \bm{P}^{\prime}_{t} is either 𝑷 t \bm{P}_{t} or f ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ) f(\bm{P}_{t}) . First, by Proposition 10 , 𝔼 ⁡ [ X t ] = 𝔼 ⁡ [ S ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ′ , Y t ) − S ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ′ , f ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ) ) ] = 0 \mathbb{E}[X_{t}]=\mathbb{E}[S(\bm{P}^{\prime}_{t},Y_{t})-S(\bm{P}^{\prime}_{t},f(\bm{P}_{t}))]=0 . Second, by assumption, sup t Var ⁡ ( S ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ′ , f ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ) ) ) &lt; ∞ . \sup_{t}\mathrm{Var}(S(\bm{P}^{\prime}_{t},f(\bm{P}_{t})))&lt;\infty. Hence, also sup t Var ⁡ ( S ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ′ , f ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ) ) ) = sup t Var ⁡ ( 𝔼 ⁡ [ S ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ′ , f ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ) ) ∣ 𝑷 t ] ) ≤ sup t Var ⁡ ( S ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ′ , f ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ) ) ) &lt; ∞ . \sup_{t}\mathrm{Var}(S(\bm{P}^{\prime}_{t},f(\bm{P}_{t})))=\sup_{t}\mathrm{Var}(\mathbb{E}[S(\bm{P}^{\prime}_{t},f(\bm{P}_{t}))\mid\bm{P}_{t}])\leq\sup_{t}\mathrm{Var}(S(\bm{P}^{\prime}_{t},f(\bm{P}_{t})))&lt;\infty. It follows that also sup t Var ⁡ ( X t ) &lt; ∞ \sup_{t}\mathrm{Var}(X_{t})&lt;\infty . Third, we know that Y t Y_{t} is independent of 𝑷 t ′ \bm{P}_{t^{\prime}} and Y t ′ Y_{t^{\prime}} for t &gt; t ′ t&gt;t^{\prime} , conditional on 𝑷 t \bm{P}_{t} . Moreover, 𝑷 t ′ \bm{P}_{t}^{\prime} is constant given 𝑷 t \bm{P}_{t} . Hence, given 𝑷 t \bm{P}_{t} , also X t = S ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ′ , Y t ) − S ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ′ , f ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ) ) X_{t}=S(\bm{P}^{\prime}_{t},Y_{t})-S(\bm{P}^{\prime}_{t},f(\bm{P}_{t})) is independent of X t ′ X_{t^{\prime}} . Moreover, 𝔼 ⁡ [ X t ∣ 𝑷 t ] = 𝔼 ⁡ [ S ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ′ , Y t ) − S ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ′ , f ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ) ) ∣ 𝑷 t ] = S ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ′ , f ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ) ) − S ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ′ , f ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ) ) = 0 . \mathbb{E}[X_{t}\mid\bm{P}_{t}]=\mathbb{E}[S(\bm{P}^{\prime}_{t},Y_{t})-S(\bm{P}^{\prime}_{t},f(\bm{P}_{t}))\mid\bm{P}_{t}]=S(\bm{P}^{\prime}_{t},f(\bm{P}_{t}))-S(\bm{P}^{\prime}_{t},f(\bm{P}_{t}))=0. It follows for t &gt; t ′ t&gt;t^{\prime} that Cov ⁡ ( X t , X t ′ ) = 𝔼 ⁡ [ X t ​ X t ′ ] = 𝔼 ⁡ [ 𝔼 ⁡ [ X t ​ X t ′ ∣ 𝑷 t ] ] = 𝔼 ⁡ [ 𝔼 ⁡ [ X t ∣ 𝑷 t ] ​ 𝔼 ​ [ X t ′ ∣ 𝑷 t ] ] = 0 . \mathrm{Cov}(X_{t},X_{t^{\prime}})=\mathbb{E}[X_{t}X_{t^{\prime}}]=\mathbb{E}[\mathbb{E}[X_{t}X_{t^{\prime}}\mid\bm{P}_{t}]]=\mathbb{E}[\mathbb{E}[X_{t}\mid\bm{P}_{t}]\mathbb{E}[X_{t^{\prime}}\mid\bm{P}_{t}]]=0. This shows all conditions of the theorem and thus lim t → ∞ 1 T ​ ∑ t = 1 T X t = 0 \lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}X_{t}=0 almost surely. Now we turn to the limit of 1 T ​ ∑ t = 1 T S ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ′ , f ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ) ) \frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}S(\bm{P}_{t}^{\prime},f(\bm{P}_{t})) . By assumption, sup t | S ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ′ , f ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ) ) | &lt; ∞ \sup_{t}|S(\bm{P}^{\prime}_{t},f(\bm{P}_{t}))|&lt;\infty , so this limit exists and is finite. Thus, almost surely lim T → ∞ 1 T ​ ∑ t = 1 T S ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ′ , f ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ) ) = lim T → ∞ 1 T ​ ∑ t = 1 T S ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ′ , Y t ) − X t \lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}S(\bm{P}^{\prime}_{t},f(\bm{P}_{t}))=\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}S(\bm{P}^{\prime}_{t},Y_{t})-X_{t} = lim T → ∞ 1 T ​ ∑ t = 1 T S ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ′ , Y t ) − lim T → ∞ 1 T ​ ∑ t = 1 T X t = lim T → ∞ 1 T ​ ∑ t = 1 T S ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ′ , Y t ) . =\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}S(\bm{P}^{\prime}_{t},Y_{t})-\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}X_{t}=\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}S(\bm{P}^{\prime}_{t},Y_{t}). Using Proposition 10 , it follows that almost surely lim T → ∞ 1 T ​ Regret ​ ( T ) = lim T → ∞ 1 T ​ ∑ t = 1 T S ⁡ ( f ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ) , Y t ) − S ⁡ ( 𝑷 t , Y t ) = lim T → ∞ 1 T ​ ∑ t = 1 T S ⁡ ( f ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ) , Y t ) − lim T → ∞ 1 T ​ ∑ t = 1 T S ⁡ ( 𝑷 t , Y t ) \lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{T}\mathrm{Regret}(T)=\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}S(f(\bm{P}_{t}),Y_{t})-S(\bm{P}_{t},Y_{t})=\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}S(f(\bm{P}_{t}),Y_{t})-\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}S(\bm{P}_{t},Y_{t}) = lim T → ∞ 1 T ​ ∑ t = 1 T S ⁡ ( f ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ) , f ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ) ) − lim T → ∞ 1 T ​ ∑ t = 1 T S ⁡ ( 𝑷 t , f ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ) ) = lim T → ∞ 1 T ​ ∑ t = 1 T S ⁡ ( f ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ) , f ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ) ) − S ⁡ ( 𝑷 t , f ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ) ) . =\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}S(f(\bm{P}_{t}),f(\bm{P}_{t}))-\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}S(\bm{P}_{t},f(\bm{P}_{t}))=\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}S(f(\bm{P}_{t}),f(\bm{P}_{t}))-S(\bm{P}_{t},f(\bm{P}_{t})). Turning to the “in particular” part, note that this limit is finite by the above, and it is nonnegative since S S is assumed to be proper. Moreover, it follows that almost surely lim sup T → ∞ 1 T ​ Regret ​ ( T ) = lim T → ∞ 1 T ​ Regret ​ ( T ) ≥ 0 . \limsup_{T\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{T}\mathrm{Regret}(T)=\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{T}\mathrm{Regret}(T)\geq 0. Thus, almost surely lim sup T → ∞ 1 T ​ Regret ​ ( T ) ≤ 0 \limsup_{T\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{T}\mathrm{Regret}(T)\leq 0 if and only if lim T → ∞ 1 T ​ ∑ t = 1 T S ⁡ ( f ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ) , f ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ) ) − S ⁡ ( 𝑷 t , f ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ) ) = 0 . \lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}S(f(\bm{P}_{t}),f(\bm{P}_{t}))-S(\bm{P}_{t},f(\bm{P}_{t}))=0. This concludes the proof. ∎ D.4.2 Sublinear regret ⇔ \Leftrightarrow sublinear prediction error Now we turn to the main result of this section. We show that given our assumptions, agents have sublinear regret if and only if their prediction error is sublinear. Note that here, we do not require the 𝑷 t \bm{P}_{t} to converge; they could also oscillate between different fixed points. Theorem 10 . Let ( 𝐏 t ) t (\bm{P}_{t})_{t} be the sequence of the agent’s predictions and S S a strictly proper scoring rule. Assume that sup t Var ⁡ ( S ⁡ ( 𝐏 t ′ , Y t ) ) &lt; ∞ \sup_{t}\mathrm{Var}(S(\bm{P}^{\prime}_{t},Y_{t}))&lt;\infty for 𝐏 t ′ ∈ { 𝐏 t , f ⁡ ( 𝐏 t ) } \bm{P}^{\prime}_{t}\in\{\bm{P}_{t},f(\bm{P}_{t})\} , and assume that there exists a closed set 𝒞 ⊆ Δ ⁡ ( 𝒩 ) \mathcal{C}\subseteq\Delta(\mathcal{N}) such that 𝐏 t ∈ 𝒞 \bm{P}_{t}\in\mathcal{C} for all t t and S ⁡ ( 𝐩 , f ⁡ ( 𝐩 ) ) S({\bm{p}},f({\bm{p}})) , S ⁡ ( f ⁡ ( 𝐩 ) , f ⁡ ( 𝐩 ) ) , S(f({\bm{p}}),f({\bm{p}})), and f ⁡ ( 𝐩 ) f({\bm{p}}) are continuous in 𝐩 {\bm{p}} at any 𝐩 ∈ 𝒞 {\bm{p}}\in\mathcal{C} . Then almost surely the agent has sublinear regret if and only if ∑ t = 1 T ‖ f ⁡ ( 𝐏 t ) − 𝐏 t ‖ \sum_{t=1}^{T}\|f(\bm{P}_{t})-\bm{P}_{t}\| is sublinear, i.e., if lim t → ∞ 1 T ​ ∑ t = 1 T ‖ f ⁡ ( 𝐏 t ) − 𝐏 t ‖ = 0 \lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\|f(\bm{P}_{t})-\bm{P}_{t}\|=0 . To show the result, we begin by proving an analytic lemma. Lemma 11 . Let φ , ψ : ℕ → [ 0 , ∞ ) \varphi,\psi\colon\mathbb{N}\rightarrow[0,\infty) and assume there exists a constant C &gt; 0 C&gt;0 such that for all t ∈ ℕ , t\in\mathbb{N}, we have ψ ⁡ ( t ) ≤ C . \psi(t)\leq C. Assume that for any ϵ &gt; 0 \epsilon&gt;0 , there exists δ &gt; 0 \delta&gt;0 such that if ψ ⁡ ( t ) &gt; ϵ \psi(t)&gt;\epsilon for any t ∈ ℕ t\in\mathbb{N} , then φ ⁡ ( t ) &gt; δ \varphi(t)&gt;\delta . Then lim T → ∞ 1 T ​ ∑ t = 1 T φ ⁡ ( t ) = 0 ⇒ lim T → ∞ 1 T ​ ∑ t = 1 T ψ ⁡ ( t ) = 0 . \lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\varphi(t)=0\Rightarrow\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\psi(t)=0. Proof. We prove the contrapositive. That is, we assume that there exists some constant c &gt; 0 c&gt;0 such that there are infinitely many T ∈ ℕ T\in\mathbb{N} such that 1 T ​ ∑ t = 1 T ψ ⁡ ( t ) &gt; c \frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\psi(t)&gt;c . Let 𝒯 \mathcal{T} be the set of such T T . We show that then there exists a constant c ′ &gt; 0 c^{\prime}&gt;0 such that for infinitely many T T , 1 T ​ ∑ t = 1 T φ ⁡ ( t ) &gt; c ′ \frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\varphi(t)&gt;c^{\prime} . Let T ∈ 𝒯 T\in\mathcal{T} . Since by assumption 1 T ​ ∑ t = 1 T ψ ⁡ ( t ) &gt; c , \frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\psi(t)&gt;c, it follows that ∑ t = 1 T ψ ⁡ ( t ) c &gt; T . \sum_{t=1}^{T}\frac{\psi(t)}{c}&gt;T. Let C ′ := max ⁡ { C , 1 / c } + 1 C^{\prime}:=\max\{C,1/c\}+1 . Since ψ ⁡ ( t ) &lt; C ′ \psi(t)&lt;C^{\prime} it must be ψ ⁡ ( t ) &gt; c 2 ​ C \psi(t)&gt;\frac{c}{2C} for more than c 2 ​ C \frac{c}{2C} fraction of the times t ≤ T t\leq T . Otherwise, it would be ∑ t = 1 T ψ ⁡ ( t ) c ≤ T ⁡ ( C c ​ c 2 ​ C + ( 1 − c 2 ​ C ) ​ ϵ 2 ​ C ) ≤ T ⁡ ( 1 2 + ϵ 2 ​ C ) &lt; T . \sum_{t=1}^{T}\frac{\psi(t)}{c}\leq T\left(\frac{C}{c}\frac{c}{2C}+\left(1-\frac{c}{2C}\right)\frac{\epsilon}{2C}\right)\leq T\left(\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\epsilon}{2C}\right)&lt;T. By assumption, this gives us a δ &gt; 0 \delta&gt;0 such that whenever ψ ⁡ ( t ) &gt; ϵ := c 2 ​ C \psi(t)&gt;\epsilon:=\frac{c}{2C} , also φ ⁡ ( t ) &gt; δ \varphi(t)&gt;\delta . In particular, this applies to at least ϵ \epsilon fraction of t ≤ T t\leq T . Hence, it follows that for any T ∈ 𝒯 T\in\mathcal{T} , ∑ t = 1 T φ ⁡ ( t ) ≥ δ ​ ϵ ​ T . \sum_{t=1}^{T}\varphi(t)\geq\delta\epsilon T. This shows that there are infinitely many T T such that 1 T ​ ∑ t = 1 T φ ⁡ ( t ) &gt; δ ​ ϵ \frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\varphi(t)&gt;\delta\epsilon and thus concludes the proof. ∎ Proof of Theorem 10 . To begin, note that since 𝒞 ⊆ Δ ⁡ ( 𝒩 ) \mathcal{C}\subseteq\Delta(\mathcal{N}) is closed and Δ ⁡ ( 𝒩 ) \Delta(\mathcal{N}) compact, also 𝒞 \mathcal{C} is compact. Hence, continuity of S ⁡ ( 𝒑 , f ⁡ ( 𝒑 ) ) S({\bm{p}},f({\bm{p}})) and S ⁡ ( f ⁡ ( 𝒑 ) , f ⁡ ( 𝒑 ) ) S(f({\bm{p}}),f({\bm{p}})) implies that both are also bounded on 𝒞 \mathcal{C} and thus sup t | S ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ′ , f ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ) ) | &lt; ∞ \sup_{t}|S(\bm{P}^{\prime}_{t},f(\bm{P}_{t}))|&lt;\infty for 𝑷 t ′ ∈ { 𝑷 t , f ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ) } \bm{P}^{\prime}_{t}\in\{\bm{P}_{t},f(\bm{P}_{t})\} . Hence, by our assumptions, the conditions for Proposition 11 are satisfied. “ ⇒ \Rightarrow ”. Assume Regret ⁡ ( T ) \mathrm{Regret}(T) is sublinear. We want to show that then ∑ t = 1 T ‖ f ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ) − 𝑷 t ‖ \sum_{t=1}^{T}\|f(\bm{P}_{t})-\bm{P}_{t}\| is sublinear. To do this, we will apply Lemma 11 . To begin, define φ ⁡ ( t ) := S ⁡ ( f ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ) , f ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ) ) − S ⁡ ( 𝑷 t , f ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ) ) \varphi(t):=S(f(\bm{P}_{t}),f(\bm{P}_{t}))-S(\bm{P}_{t},f(\bm{P}_{t})) and note that φ ⁡ ( t ) ≥ 0 \varphi(t)\geq 0 since S S is proper. By Proposition 11 , it follows that if Regret ⁡ ( T ) \mathrm{Regret}(T) is sublinear, also ∑ t = 1 T φ ⁡ ( t ) \sum_{t=1}^{T}\varphi(t) is sublinear almost surely. For brevity, we omit the “almost surely” qualification in the following. Next, define ψ ⁡ ( t ) := ‖ f ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ) − 𝑷 t ‖ \psi(t):=\|f(\bm{P}_{t})-\bm{P}_{t}\| , and note that 0 ≤ ψ ⁡ ( t ) ≤ n 0\leq\psi(t)\leq n . Next, let ϵ &gt; 0 \epsilon&gt;0 arbitrary. To apply Lemma 11 to φ \varphi and ψ \psi , it remains to show that there exists δ &gt; 0 \delta&gt;0 such that whenever ψ ⁡ ( t ) ≥ ϵ \psi(t)\geq\epsilon , then φ ⁡ ( t ) ≥ δ \varphi(t)\geq\delta . To that end, let δ := min { 𝒑 ∈ 𝒞 ∣ ‖ 𝒑 − f ⁡ ( 𝒑 ) ‖ ≥ ϵ } ⁡ S ⁡ ( f ⁡ ( 𝒑 ) , f ⁡ ( 𝒑 ) ) − S ⁡ ( 𝒑 , f ⁡ ( 𝒑 ) ) . \delta:=\min_{\{{\bm{p}}\in\mathcal{C}\mid\|{\bm{p}}-f({\bm{p}})\|\geq\epsilon\}}S(f({\bm{p}}),f({\bm{p}}))-S({\bm{p}},f({\bm{p}})). Since ∥ ⋅ ∥ \|\cdot\| is continuous and f f is continuous at any 𝒑 ∈ 𝒞 {\bm{p}}\in\mathcal{C} , the set { 𝒑 ∈ 𝒞 ∣ ‖ 𝒑 − f ⁡ ( 𝒑 ) ‖ ≥ ϵ } \{{\bm{p}}\in\mathcal{C}\mid\|{\bm{p}}-f({\bm{p}})\|\geq\epsilon\} is closed and thus compact. Moreover, S ⁡ ( f ⁡ ( 𝒑 ) , f ⁡ ( 𝒑 ) ) S(f({\bm{p}}),f({\bm{p}})) and S ⁡ ( 𝒑 , f ⁡ ( 𝒑 ) ) S({\bm{p}},f({\bm{p}})) are continuous by assumption, and thus the minimum is attained at some point 𝒑 ^ ∈ 𝒞 \hat{{\bm{p}}}\in\mathcal{C} . But since S S is strictly proper, it follows δ = S ⁡ ( f ⁡ ( 𝒑 ^ ) , f ⁡ ( 𝒑 ^ ) ) − S ⁡ ( 𝒑 ^ , f ⁡ ( 𝒑 ^ ) ) &gt; 0 . \delta=S(f(\hat{{\bm{p}}}),f(\hat{{\bm{p}}}))-S(\hat{{\bm{p}}},f(\hat{{\bm{p}}}))&gt;0. Hence, since 𝑷 t ∈ 𝒞 \bm{P}_{t}\in\mathcal{C} for any t ∈ ℕ , t\in\mathbb{N}, it follows that whenever φ ⁡ ( t ) ≥ ϵ , \varphi(t)\geq\epsilon, it follows φ ⁡ ( t ) = S ⁡ ( f ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ) , f ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ) ) − S ⁡ ( 𝑷 t , f ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ) ) ≥ δ . \varphi(t)=S(f(\bm{P}_{t}),f(\bm{P}_{t}))-S(\bm{P}_{t},f(\bm{P}_{t}))\geq\delta. This shows all conditions for Lemma 11 . Hence, we conclude that lim t → ∞ 1 T ​ ∑ t = 1 T ‖ f ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ) − 𝑷 t ‖ = 0 \lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\|f(\bm{P}_{t})-\bm{P}_{t}\|=0 . “ ⇐ \Leftarrow ”. Let φ ⁡ ( t ) := ‖ f ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ) − 𝑷 t ‖ \varphi(t):=\|f(\bm{P}_{t})-\bm{P}_{t}\| and ψ := S ⁡ ( f ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ) , f ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ) ) − S ⁡ ( 𝑷 t , f ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ) ) \psi:=S(f(\bm{P}_{t}),f(\bm{P}_{t}))-S(\bm{P}_{t},f(\bm{P}_{t})) . We assume that ∑ t = 1 T φ ⁡ ( t ) \sum_{t=1}^{T}\varphi(t) is sublinear in T T and want to show that then Regret ⁡ ( T ) \mathrm{Regret}(T) is sublinear as well. To do so, we will show that ∑ t = 1 T ψ ⁡ ( t ) \sum_{t=1}^{T}\psi(t) is sublinear using our lemma, and then the required statement follows again from Proposition 11 . Now we have to show the conditions of the lemma. First, as before, φ ⁡ ( t ) ≥ 0 \varphi(t)\geq 0 and ψ ⁡ ( t ) ≥ 0 . \psi(t)\geq 0. Second, as noted in the beginning, we have sup t ψ ⁡ ( t ) &lt; ∞ \sup_{t}\psi(t)&lt;\infty by our assumption that S ⁡ ( f ⁡ ( 𝒑 ) , f ⁡ ( 𝒑 ) ) S(f({\bm{p}}),f({\bm{p}})) and S ⁡ ( 𝒑 , f ⁡ ( 𝒑 ) ) S({\bm{p}},f({\bm{p}})) are continuous on 𝒞 . \mathcal{C}. Now let ϵ &gt; 0 \epsilon&gt;0 arbitrary. Assume that S ⁡ ( f ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ) , f ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ) ) − S ⁡ ( 𝑷 t , f ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ) ) &gt; ϵ S(f(\bm{P}_{t}),f(\bm{P}_{t}))-S(\bm{P}_{t},f(\bm{P}_{t}))&gt;\epsilon for some ϵ &gt; 0 \epsilon&gt;0 and t ∈ ℕ . t\in\mathbb{N}. Consider the set 𝒞 ′ := { 𝒑 ∈ 𝒞 ∣ S ⁡ ( f ⁡ ( 𝒑 ) , f ⁡ ( 𝒑 ) ) − S ⁡ ( 𝒑 , f ⁡ ( 𝒑 ) ) ≥ ϵ } \mathcal{C}^{\prime}:=\{{\bm{p}}\in\mathcal{C}\mid S(f({\bm{p}}),f({\bm{p}}))-S({\bm{p}},f({\bm{p}}))\geq\epsilon\} . Since S ⁡ ( f ⁡ ( 𝒑 ) , f ⁡ ( 𝒑 ) ) S(f({\bm{p}}),f({\bm{p}})) and S ⁡ ( 𝒑 , f ⁡ ( 𝒑 ) ) S({\bm{p}},f({\bm{p}})) are continuous on 𝒞 \mathcal{C} by assumption, this set is compact. Moreover, the function 𝒑 ∈ 𝒞 ↦ ‖ 𝒑 − f ⁡ ( 𝒑 ) ‖ {\bm{p}}\in\mathcal{C}\mapsto\|{\bm{p}}-f({\bm{p}})\| is continuous since f f is continuous on 𝒞 \mathcal{C} by assumption. Hence, the minimum δ := min 𝒑 ∈ 𝒞 ′ ⁡ ‖ 𝒑 − f ⁡ ( 𝒑 ) ‖ \delta:=\min_{{\bm{p}}\in\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}\|{\bm{p}}-f({\bm{p}})\| is attained at some point 𝒑 ^ ∈ 𝒞 ′ . \hat{{\bm{p}}}\in\mathcal{C}^{\prime}. Now, if δ = 0 , \delta=0, we would have 𝒑 ^ = f ⁡ ( 𝒑 ^ ) \hat{{\bm{p}}}=f(\hat{{\bm{p}}}) and thus S ⁡ ( f ⁡ ( 𝒑 ^ ) , f ⁡ ( 𝒑 ^ ) ) − S ⁡ ( 𝒑 ^ , f ⁡ ( 𝒑 ^ ) ) = S ⁡ ( 𝒑 ^ , 𝒑 ^ ) − S ⁡ ( 𝒑 ^ , 𝒑 ^ ) = 0 &lt; ϵ , S(f(\hat{{\bm{p}}}),f(\hat{{\bm{p}}}))-S(\hat{{\bm{p}}},f(\hat{{\bm{p}}}))=S(\hat{{\bm{p}}},\hat{{\bm{p}}})-S(\hat{{\bm{p}}},\hat{{\bm{p}}})=0&lt;\epsilon, which is a contradiction. Hence, δ &gt; 0 . \delta&gt;0. Since 𝑷 t ∈ 𝒞 , \bm{P}_{t}\in\mathcal{C}, it follows from S ⁡ ( f ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ) , f ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ) ) − S ⁡ ( 𝑷 t , f ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ) ) ≥ ϵ , S(f(\bm{P}_{t}),f(\bm{P}_{t}))-S(\bm{P}_{t},f(\bm{P}_{t}))\geq\epsilon, for t ∈ ℕ t\in\mathbb{N} that 𝑷 t ∈ 𝒞 ′ \bm{P}_{t}\in\mathcal{C}^{\prime} and thus ‖ 𝑷 t − f ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ) ‖ ≥ δ . \|\bm{P}_{t}-f(\bm{P}_{t})\|\geq\delta. This shows the third condition for the lemma. We can thus conclude that lim T → ∞ 1 T ​ ∑ t = 1 T ψ ⁡ ( t ) = 0 . \lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\psi(t)=0. Using Proposition 11 , this concludes the proof. ∎ D.4.3 Convergence to fixed points The next result shows that if the agent’s predictions converge to some distribution 𝒑 {\bm{p}} , then 𝒑 {\bm{p}} must be a fixed point. Corollary 1 . In addition to the assumptions from Theorem 10 , assume that 𝐏 t \bm{P}_{t} converges almost surely to a limit lim t → ∞ 𝐏 t = 𝐩 ∗ \lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}\bm{P}_{t}={\bm{p}}^{*} . Then almost surely 𝐩 ∗ {\bm{p}}^{*} is a fixed point if and only if the agent has sublinear regret. Proof. By Theorem 10 , almost surely the agent has sublinear regret if and only if lim t → ∞ 1 T ​ ∑ t = 1 T ‖ f ⁡ ( 𝐏 t ) − 𝐏 t ‖ = 0 . \lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\|f(\mathbf{P}_{t})-\mathbf{P}_{t}\|=0. It remains to show that, given that the 𝑷 t \bm{P}_{t} converge, the latter is equivalent to convergence to a fixed point. Since 𝒞 \mathcal{C} is compact and 𝑷 t ∈ 𝒞 \bm{P}_{t}\in\mathcal{C} for all t ∈ ℕ , t\in\mathbb{N}, also 𝒑 ∗ ∈ 𝒞 . {\bm{p}}^{*}\in\mathcal{C}. Hence, f f is continuous at 𝒑 ∗ , {\bm{p}}^{*}, so ‖ f ⁡ ( 𝒑 ∗ ) − 𝒑 ∗ ‖ = ‖ f ⁡ ( lim t → ∞ 𝑷 t ) − lim t → ∞ 𝑷 t ‖ = lim t → ∞ ‖ f ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ) − 𝑷 t ‖ . \|f({\bm{p}}^{*})-{\bm{p}}^{*}\|=\left\|f\left(\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}\bm{P}_{t}\right)-\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}\bm{P}_{t}\right\|=\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}\|f(\bm{P}_{t})-\bm{P}_{t}\|. Since this sequence converges, it is equal to its Cesàro mean, lim t → ∞ ‖ f ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ) − 𝑷 t ‖ = lim T → ∞ 1 T ​ ∑ t = 1 T ‖ f ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ) − 𝑷 t ‖ . \lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}\|f(\bm{P}_{t})-\bm{P}_{t}\|=\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\|f(\bm{P}_{t})-\bm{P}_{t}\|. Hence, ‖ f ⁡ ( 𝒑 ∗ ) − 𝒑 ∗ ‖ = lim t → ∞ ‖ f ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ) − 𝑷 t ‖ = lim T → ∞ 1 T ​ ∑ t = 1 T ‖ f ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ) − 𝑷 t ‖ . \|f({\bm{p}}^{*})-{\bm{p}}^{*}\|=\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}\|f(\bm{P}_{t})-\bm{P}_{t}\|=\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\|f(\bm{P}_{t})-\bm{P}_{t}\|. It follows that, if lim t → ∞ 𝑷 t = 𝒑 ∗ , \lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}\bm{P}_{t}={\bm{p}}^{*}, then ‖ f ⁡ ( 𝒑 ∗ ) − 𝒑 ∗ ‖ = 0 ⇔ lim T → ∞ 1 T ​ ∑ t = 1 T ‖ f ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ) − 𝑷 t ‖ = 0 . \|f({\bm{p}}^{*})-{\bm{p}}^{*}\|=0\Leftrightarrow\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\|f(\bm{P}_{t})-\bm{P}_{t}\|=0. This shows that, almost surely, 𝒑 ∗ {\bm{p}}^{*} is a fixed point, if and only if ∑ t = 1 T ‖ f ⁡ ( 𝑷 t ) − 𝑷 t ‖ \sum_{t=1}^{T}\|f(\bm{P}_{t})-\bm{P}_{t}\| is sublinear. ∎ D.5 Prediction markets Lastly, we consider prediction markets. We assume a simplified model of a prediction market, in which traders submit a single prediction and get scored using a proper scoring rule. The prediction that is output by the market and that influences the outcome is just a weighted average of the individual traders’ predictions. In this situation, if a trader has a small weight and can thus barely influence the market prediction, the trader’s score will mostly be determined by the accuracy of the report, rather than the influence of the report on the market. Thus, if all traders are small relative to the market, the equilibrium prediction will be close to a fixed point. A similar result was shown by Hardt et al. 2022 in the performative prediction context. They define a firm’s performative power as the degree to which the firm can influence the overall outcome with their prediction. Hardt et al. 2022 show that in an equilibrium, the distance between a player’s (performatively optimal) equilibrium strategy and their strategy when optimizing loss against the fixed equilibrium distribution (here, this means predicting the market probability) is bounded by the power of the trader. We give an analogous result for our formal setting and assumptions. To formalize the setting, assume that there are N N players. We associate with each player n ∈ [ N ] n\in[N] a number w n ∈ [ 0 , 1 ] w_{n}\in[0,1] s.t. ∑ n w n = 1 \sum_{n}w_{n}=1 , representing, intuitively, what fraction of the overall capital in the market is provided by player n n . In the game, all players simultaneously submit a probability distribution 𝒑 n {\bm{p}}_{n} . Then the event Y Y is sampled according to the distribution 𝒒 = f ⁡ ( ∑ n w n ​ 𝒑 n ) \bm{q}=f(\sum_{n}w_{n}{\bm{p}}_{n}) . Finally, each player is scored in proportion to S ⁡ ( 𝒑 n , Y ) S({\bm{p}}_{n},Y) for some strictly proper scoring rule S S . Typical market scoring rules would consider terms like S ⁡ ( 𝒑 n , Y ) − S ⁡ ( 𝒑 n , Y ) S({\bm{p}}_{n},Y)-S({\bm{p}}_{n},Y) , but subtracting S ⁡ ( 𝒑 n , Y ) S({\bm{p}}_{n},Y) (or multiplying by constants) does not matter for the game. We assume that players maximize their expected score, 𝔼 ⁡ [ S ⁡ ( 𝒑 n , Y ) ] = S ⁡ ( 𝒑 n , f ⁡ ( ∑ m w m ​ 𝒑 m ) ) \mathbb{E}[S({\bm{p}}_{n},Y)]=S({\bm{p}}_{n},f(\sum_{m}w_{m}{\bm{p}}_{m})) . For discussions of market scoring rules, see Hanson 2003 and Pennock and Sami 2007 . Prior work has connected these market scoring rules to more realistic prediction markets that trade Arrow–Debreu securities markets such as PredictIt [e.g., Hanson 2003 ; Pennock and Sami 2007 , Section 4; Chen and Pennock 2007 ; Agrawal et al. 2009 ]. We assume that f f is common knowledge. Moreover, in the following we only consider pure strategy equilibria, and we do not investigate the existence of equilibria. Theorem 11 . Let S S be a proper scoring rule and let G , g G,g as in the Gneiting and Raftery characterization of S S . Let ( 𝐩 n ) n ({\bm{p}}_{n})_{n} be a pure strategy Nash equilibrium of the aforedefined game and let 𝐩 ^ := ∑ n w n ​ 𝐩 n \hat{{\bm{p}}}:=\sum_{n}w_{n}{\bm{p}}_{n} be the market prediction. Assume f f is differentiable at 𝐩 ^ \hat{{\bm{p}}} . In particular, this theorem shows that players n n with very low w n w_{n} (little capital/influence on 𝒒 \bm{q} ) will accurately predict 𝒒 = f ⁡ ( 𝒑 ^ ) \bm{q}=f(\hat{{\bm{p}}}) . Note, however, that 𝒑 ^ \hat{{\bm{p}}} is not necessarily a fixed point or close to a fixed point. If there are are also players n n with very high w n w_{n} , then their prediction and the overall market prediction may be wrong. (So interestingly the overall market probability 𝒑 ^ = ∑ n w n ​ 𝒑 n \hat{{\bm{p}}}=\sum_{n}w_{n}{\bm{p}}_{n} is worse than the prediction of individuals. One might take this to suggest that anyone interested in 𝒒 \bm{q} should look at the latter type of predictions. Of course, if this is what everyone does, it is not so clear anymore that the model 𝒒 = f ⁡ ( ∑ n w n ​ 𝒑 n ) \bm{q}=f(\sum_{n}w_{n}{\bm{p}}_{n}) is accurate.) Proof. In addition to the assumptions from Theorem 11 , assume that f f is Lipschitz-continuous and C := sup p ∈ Δ ⁡ ( 𝒩 ) ‖ g ⁡ ( 𝐩 ) ‖ γ 𝐩 &lt; ∞ . Then there exists a δ &gt; 0 \delta&gt;0 such that if for all n n , w n &lt; δ , w_{n}&lt;\delta, all of 𝐩 n {\bm{p}}_{n} and f ⁡ ( 𝐩 n ) f({\bm{p}}_{n}) , for all i i , as well as ∑ m w m ​ 𝐩 m \sum_{m}w_{m}{\bm{p}}_{m} and f ⁡ ( ∑ m w m ​ 𝐩 m ) f(\sum_{m}w_{m}{\bm{p}}_{m}) are within ϵ \epsilon of each other. For example, it is unclear what happens when players make predictions repeatedly . (To keep things simple, one should probably still imagine that all players know f f and that the environment probability is determined by f f applied to the majority forecast. If the traders have private information, prediction markets become harder to analyze. For some discussions, see Ostrovsky 2009 , Chen and Waggoner 2016 .) ◄ Feeling lucky?
